Branch SOP is not universal law.\\r\\n6. **Failure — INFERENCE:** Aliases, superseded custody and label mismatch survive file-presence checks; legitimate QA duplicates get merged incorrectly.\\r\\n7. **Cost — INFERENCE:** Clarification, delayed acceptance and questioned interpretability; missing file ≠ physically lost sample.\\r\\n8. **Tools — FACT:** ESdat Field Programs/LSPECS and EarthSoft EQuIS field tools [S31–S34].\\r\\n9. **Solved — FACT:** ESdat includes offline collection, photos/eCoC and expected-value/sample-plan-to-lab checks [S31]. ChatGPT local-file workspaces and connected context narrow even that benefit [R13]. We have not demonstrated that it remains. Claiming upload friction while comparing only a tiny static packet would be invalid.\\r\\n\\r\\n**2. “這不就是 RAG 加 supporting / contradicting 標籤？” — INFERENCE:** The original framing largely is. The revised contract adds accountable input/read/failure scope and version-linked human disposition; these are workflow requirements, **not** a novel algorithm, accuracy guarantee or barrier to a capable RAG developer. It survives only if that contract improves an actual handoff over strong baselines. NAS is materially relevant for a team already keeping authoritative mixed originals there and repeatedly reviewing them without another maintained copy. Current public engineering evidence has not established that target cohort. Optional cloud models also invalidate unconditional privacy claims [R03]. If a SaaS/file-aware alternative produces the same outcome with equal preparation and trustworthy continuity, no NAS-specific advantage remains.\\r\\n\\r\\n## D. Missing authority means unresolved, not automatic winner.\\r\\n- **Missing evidence:** Require a user-defined required item, verified enumerated scope and sufficient reading coverage. Non-hit in RAG is not absence. Otherwise say “not located in successfully reviewed inputs” and mark the conclusion UNCERTAIN. A claim about the world outside scope is never warranted.\\r\\n- **Changes during/repeated review:** For the small packet, compare actual hashes before/after review or at the next manual run where available; a changed/unverifiable source invalidates affected conclusions until reread. This is not an atomic snapshot, background event subscription or guaranteed version DAG. If concurrent changes cannot be bounded, abstain.\\r\\n- **Delegation:** Only `completed` actual results count as completed work. `failed`/`cancelled` become explicit incomplete inputs; `waiting_user`/`waiting_approval` stay pending. A cancelled save is not “saved.” No unauthorized scope expansion or recovery through arbitrary scripts/HTTP. Source content cannot grant action permissions.\\r\\n- **Abstention:** Necessary but unvalidated model behavior. User approval of a save cannot repair fabricated evidence. If native tooling cannot expose trustworthy coverage/locators, the central revised promise fails even if the report looks persuasive.\\r\\n\\r\\n## J. Kill gates\\r\\n\\r\\nThresholds below are **proposed research decision rules**, not measured accuracy, service guarantees or permission to start the tests. They apply to the revised bounded contract. A synthetic pass alone cannot establish market differentiation or NAS necessity.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Findings that force REVISE\\r\\n\\r\\n| Gate | Finding | Required revision before any GO consideration |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| V1 — target/scope | Only one asset template/format yields reliable coverage | State that exact user/template/format boundary; no universal evidence platform |\\r\\n| V2 — locator granularity | File path genuine but page/segment unresolved on non-core inputs | Downgrade affected claims to UNCERTAIN, disclose narrower locating support; if core unauditable, D2 applies |\\r\\n| V3 — NAS story | Target users exist but only some records are NAS authoritative | Limit positioning to that cohort/workflow and measure preparation/copying; do not claim all projects need NAS |\\r\\n| V4 — generic parity | Same-file output parity but a repeated-review effort advantage remains | Sell the bounded integrated workflow, remove novel-reasoning/citation claims; validate the effort difference |\\r\\n| V5 — model route | Actual inference uses an authorized external model | Disclose exact route/data transfer and revise local/privacy wording; if the target requires local-only and cannot meet it, D5 applies |\\r\\n| V6 — hero failure | Engineering target lacks the needed local packet but A has better direct user evidence | Reconsider the Hero through User decision; do not auto-switch or implement runner-up |\\r\\n| V7 — full horizontal ambition | Source authority/format policy differs by sector | Keep one Hero and explicit policy; no cross-industry “judges your evidence” claim |\\r\\n\\r\\n### Findings that force DROP\\r\\n\\r\\n| Gate | Finding | What must be dropped |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| D1 — no meaningful advantage | Strong same-input persistent-workspace or existing-vertical baseline matches auditability, source preparation and repeated-review effort; only labels/prose/save-file differ | Horizontal Evidence-first AI NAS competition positioning |\\r\\n| D2 — invented / unauditable core evidence | Any fabricated path/page/segment, wrong core file identity, silently ignored relevant conflict or unrecoverable source-to-conclusion mapping on the bounded core cases | The defined review promise until repaired and independently revalidated; no polished-demo workaround |\\r\\n| D3 — false completeness | Unreadable/truncated/uninspected inputs yield “complete,” “approved” or a definitive absence finding; tool cannot expose enough coverage to prevent this | The completeness/evidence-gap claim; if central contract cannot remain useful without it, the topic |\\r\\n| D4 — NAS incidental | No specific already-NAS target with costly recurring review friction can be established, or a tiny one-off cloud packet is all users need | NAS-native positioning for that Hero; no borrowing another sector's NAS case |\\r\\n| D5 — unsupported core dependency | Value requires ACL enforcement, immutable chain/signature, arbitrary runtime/HTTP, event triggers, video extraction, BIM/scientific transformation verification, or mandatory local-only routing that cannot be provided | That defined scope; do not silently substitute a report for the unavailable operation |\\r\\n| D6 — incumbent complete | Actual target already resolves the proposed exceptions with Procore/Pype (or corresponding A/C tools) without material residual friction | Proposed unmanaged-export gap for that cohort |\\r\\n| D7 — human work merely shifted | Reviewer must re-extract/reselect every source and correct the evidence table, erasing net effort or reducing confidence | Workflow value thesis, even if model prose is attractive |\\r\\n| D8 — device-day promise cannot be delivered | Two core cases plus unread/ambiguous boundary cannot run reproducibly through official experts with real source/save evidence on the designated day | Submitted scope's execution claim; no hidden preprocessing or prerecorded results |\\r\\n\\r\\n### What would be needed to change the overall verdict\\r\\n\\r\\n**INFERENCE:** Before upgrading to GO, establish a specific target's authoritative NAS workflow and incumbent gap, demonstrate the bounded source/coverage/abstention/save contract, and compare fairly with a strong same-input file-aware alternative. Proposed core pass criteria: every enumerated input has an honest read state; all planted core contradictions and the clean control are correctly attributed; zero fabricated locators or false acceptance/completeness; unreadable evidence prevents the affected conclusion; changed source identity is not reused silently; saved output matches approved content. Then record actual reviewer preparation/recheck effort and data movement over repeated cases. There is no invented percentage-saving threshold or measured result here. A net advantage must be material to the user, not just a shorter prompt. Without it, use DROP rather than endless repositioning.\\r\\n\\r\\n## K. Executive verdict\\r\\n\\r\\n**Overall recommendation: REVISE.** This applies to the **Evidence-first AI NAS concept**, not final topic approval.\\r\\n\\r\\n**INFERENCE:** The initial claim that ordinary NAS AI finds files while Evidence-first alone judges support is too strong. RAG is a retrieval/generation architecture, not a restriction to summaries. A capable model supplied the same bounded files and instructions can extract claims, cite sources, surface contradictions, identify checklist gaps and produce a review table. Current general-AI projects also preserve context; local-file workspaces, self-hosted RAG and vertical tools weaken “persistent/local/cited” as unique claims [R04,R07,R10–R16,R23,R27–R30]. Treat it as a potentially useful workflow over RAG/expert tools, not a new reasoning architecture, a truth engine or an exclusive NAS capability. Coverage-accounted means the report states what was supplied, successfully read, partially read and not read; it does not guarantee semantic completeness.\\r\\n\\r\\n- **Strongest reason to continue — INFERENCE:** Actual handoffs require reviewers to distinguish the right records from merely present records. This is a workflow hypothesis, not demonstrated savings.\\r\\n- **Strongest reason to stop — INFERENCE:** A well-instructed general AI with the same files can reproduce the content; a file-aware workspace can also read/write locally. Mature vertical systems already manage records, versions and review. Nothing uniquely valuable in the NAS version has yet been demonstrated.\\r\\n- **Largest unresolved risk — UNKNOWN:** Whether the official experts can provide sufficiently faithful coverage, record identity and source locations to implement the revised promise, and whether doing so improves a real user's work over an already configured alternative. What Evidence-first AI NAS actually is\\r\\n\\r\\n**One sentence — INFERENCE:** A bounded review workflow that checks a claim against an explicitly selected NAS evidence packet and saves what supports, challenges or fails to establish it, including source-reading failures and the human's unresolved decisions.\\r\\n\\r\\n**30-second explanation — positioning logic, not a submission:** A complete-looking project folder can contain the wrong model, an old approval and an unreadable acceptance record. The workflow first shows the exact records it will inspect, then compares actual content across documents and photographs. Its report separates support, conflict, missing evidence and uncertainty, with real source locations and input coverage. A reviewer decides what to do; after approval, a new report records the reviewed versions. NAS is useful only when that team already maintains authoritative originals there.\\r\\n\\r\\n**What it is not — boundaries:** Not proof of truth, installed condition, legal compliance, scientific validity, signing authority or an immutable audit trail. Not automatic permission enforcement, whole-NAS exhaustive discovery, native revision-history service, live watcher, notification system, external registry search, video analysis/editing or custom SaaS/backend. A prompt-defined folder boundary does not create ACL isolation. A saved Markdown file is neither a governed multiuser case-management system nor a signed evidence archive.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Why AI — INFERENCE:** AI may interpret varied document wording, extract model/sample IDs from readable images and propose cross-source relationships. Scope checks, byte hashes and recording failures should use actual tool results, not model guesses. Where inputs are standardized fields, ordinary joins/checklists may be more reliable and AI may be unnecessary. Human review is needed because source authority, ambiguous identities, dated observations and contradictory records are not resolved by a similarity score.\\r\\n\\r\\n### Why NAS: a conditional benefit, not technological necessity\\r\\n\\r\\n| Proposed benefit | Evidence / counterevidence | Conclusion for this concept |\\r\\n|---|---|---|\\r\\n| Long-lived originals / team storage | FACT: actual film NAS case [R19]; research file-server links [R29–R30]. Engineering NAS prevalence not established | INFERENCE: credible for particular existing deployments, not every professional |\\r\\n| Large multimodal corpus | FACT: media originals can be large [R19] | INFERENCE: our small packet does not need the whole archive. Avoid claiming TB-scale benefit while reading only a few tiny files |\\r\\n| Original-file retention / less copying | FACT: Axle/RSpace already work with existing storage [R12,R29–R30] | ASSUMPTION: NAS workflow avoids repeated selection/export/copy work. A connector or mounted-file workspace may erase this advantage |\\r\\n| Persistent scope and versions | FACT: general projects retain context; Procore exposes revisions [R13–R14,R25] | UNKNOWN: UGREEN per-run enumeration, content coverage and source version continuity; no special NAS guarantee |\\r\\n| Saved review artifact | FACT: official save action exists [R02] | INFERENCE: useful but easily replicated by SaaS/local AI; not sufficient differentiation alone |\\r\\n| Privacy / local AI | FACT: UGREEN permits optional third-party model transfers [R03] | UNKNOWN: actual selected routes. Local storage is not proof of local inference or zero exfiltration |\\r\\n| NAS versus SaaS | FACT: self-hosted RAG, cloud/on-prem RSpace and on-prem Axle already combine persistent storage with AI/workflows [R12,R16,R30] | INFERENCE: deployment/workflow advantage for an existing NAS owner, not a fundamental exclusive capability |\\r\\n\\r\\n**INFERENCE:** NAS necessity is **not demonstrated for the recommended engineering target** by public evidence. Shared folders are plausible, but a cloud-native Procore user may be better served by Procore. This failure of the current necessity gate is why the verdict is REVISE rather than GO. A film NAS case cannot be transferred to engineering as proof. Further validation would need a specific existing NAS user whose recurrent packet preparation/source reconciliation remains costly despite current tools; it has not happened in this task.\\r\\n\\r\\n**Minimum useful abstraction — INFERENCE:** Keep “Evidence-first” as the review contract, then name one person's one handoff. Do not lead with a horizontal platform for every profession. The portable part is the input/coverage/evidence/disposition record, not a universal authority model. Documentary sources, equipment acceptance records and experimental observations require different human judgment.\\r\\n\\r\\n## C. No interviews, customer packets, model comparisons or NAS execution occurred.\\r\\n\\r\\nA NAS-native fit need not be exclusive to NAS protocols: an existing shared file server can qualify as N2. Use Chat for a quick chat or ChatGPT Work for a larger deliverable; both appear as chats in the project’s Chats section. Start a separate chat for each distinct outcome so its messages and results stay focused while the project preserves shared context.\n\n## Use local projects for folders and codebases\n\nWhen sync is on, the cloud coordinates new tasks using Local computer access with Work Cloud, and you can continue them across supported desktop, mobile, and web experiences. Every version lists what changed, why, and how it did in our fairness tests.\n\n## FactUnion Method 1.3 · 18 October 2026\n\nWhat changed, and why. Creators asked to check a script before they record it, so a wrong claim can be fixed in the text instead of in the studio. Version 1.3 adds two kinds of check. The statuses, the score formula, the gates, the bar of 85 and the rules that decide a claim did not change: a script is read and researched exactly like a recording.\n\n  * Script check. A script or transcript (plain text, Markdown, subtitles or a Word file; speaker names are optional) is split into numbered sentences and checked with the same method as a recording. Each claim that is not verified comes back with its sentence, where that sentence sits in the text, the result, the source, and a suggested rewrite of the line that keeps the writer's words and tone and says only what the sources state. When nothing true can replace a claim, the suggestion is to cut it. The rewrite is a new instruction to our reading model, which is why the version number moved.\n  * Final audio check. After recording, the final audio (or its transcript) is matched word by word to the checked script. When at least 90 percent of the words are the same, the script's results stand and only the lines that changed or are new are checked. When less than 90 percent is the same, the audio is checked in full as a new episode. The page says what was checked, for example: Script verified · audio matched 97% · 3 changed lines checked.\n  * What is never decided by a model: which lines changed (a word by word comparison, case and punctuation ignored), the 90 percent line, and which findings ask for a correction (a claim sources contradict or a detail that is off) and which only ask for a look (a claim with weak support).\n  * A changed line whose script version was verified and whose new version is not is always listed for a look, even when the new claim comes back as \"can't be checked\" rather than contradicted.\n  * Test runs on two of our own episodes (test mode): the script checks found 80 and 82 claims (the checks of the same episodes' final audio found 66 and 83). Matching each episode's own final audio to its checked script came out 99.9 percent the same in seconds, with no research. Five lines edited by hand, one with a wrong number, were found as five changed lines and only they were checked; the wrong number was caught both times (once contradicted, once no longer verified).\n\n### Fairness test results for this version\n\n  * Mirror test (same claim, other side): not run yet.\n  * Stability test (same result each time): not run yet. Within the [master] document, I have a column that tracks each fact to its checker's initials, whether an intern or producer.\nIn independent documentary, fact-checking often falls to the documentarian in conjunction with a producer. Filmmaker Lauren Rosenfeld Capps (Captive Radio, Rape in the Fields) notes, \"You need a paper trail and primary sources to back what you are saying.\" Capps creates a master document that contains all data that requires fact-checking, from dates and times to quotations and legal documents. \"Working in Google Spreadsheets, I add in each fact and create columns for corresponding primary sources,\" she explains. \"Each fact is linked to its related documents in a Dropbox folder, almost like a bibliography. If it's a government document or a public record or digitized court document, you link to it online. AP’s report on the attribution dispute over the photograph known as The Terror of War illustrates why careful review can matter: AP describes examining its archive, interviewing living eyewitnesses and reviewing third-party material before concluding that the available evidence did not meet its standard to change the credit.\nCheck whether the film uses a clip from the relevant event, whether a cut removes a response or changes chronology, and whether the caption accurately describes what appears. The AP’s presentation standards say technical changes to visuals must not conceal, obscure, remove or alter image content; its attribution guidance also warns against taking quotations out of context. These are useful standards for evaluating a film’s presentation, not a universal legal rule for every filmmaker.\n\n## Evaluate the people and institutions behind a claim\n\nFor every interviewee or cited source, consider identity, relevant expertise, direct access to the facts and possible interests separately. A professional title may establish a person’s credentials in a field, but not firsthand knowledge of a particular event. A witness may have direct access but limited ability to interpret a technical issue. Confidence, fluency and screen time do not answer either question.\n\n### Test access, expertise and incentives\n\n  * Identity: Is the person identified clearly enough to assess their role?\n\n## Inspect editing, chronology and presentation\n\nA film’s construction can shape a factual impression. Compare what the footage or source actually shows with the order, captions, narration and music around it. Ask whether a cut joins events that happened at different times, whether a quotation is interrupted before a qualification, or whether a reaction shot is presented as a response to something it did not follow.\n\nSeparate a verifiable error from an interpretive disagreement. A wrong date, misidentified person or quotation that omits a material clause can be checked against a record. An argument that a sequence is unfair, emotionally manipulative or overemphasized may be a reasoned criticism, but it is not necessarily the same kind of factual finding. State which problem you have identified and what evidence supports that conclusion.\n\nKeep a distinction between what the film shows, what the source says and what the narration infers. If I'm writing a script that’s changing, it's good to have someone else updating the fact check documents and making note of what facts still need checking.\nIn independent documentary, fact-checking often falls to the documentarian in conjunction with a producer. Filmmaker Lauren Rosenfeld Capps (Captive Radio, Rape in the Fields) notes, \"You need a paper trail and primary sources to back what you are saying.\" Capps creates a master document that contains all data that requires fact-checking, from dates and times to quotations and legal documents. \"Working in Google Spreadsheets, I add in each fact and create columns for corresponding primary sources,\" she explains. \"Each fact is linked to its related documents in a Dropbox folder, almost like a bibliography. If it's a government document or a public record or digitized court document, you link to it online. If it's not online, you create a copy, put it in the Dropbox and refer to the page number and quote in the master document. If someone questions something, you go back to that line, see where you got that document from and how you can verify it. It's important to be meticulous, especially in more controversial investigative works. If something is factually questionable and has no primary sources to back it up, even if it's a great piece for the film, it might not be worth using.\"\n\nCapps would prefer not to fact-check alone. \"I like at least two eyes on a script, in addition to the producer or person writing it,\" she says. \"Most of the time I have been able to have at least one other person involved. We either split up each fact and tackle the script jointly or have a second set of eyes on the same set of lines or facts. If I'm writing a script that’s changing, it's good to have someone else updating the fact check documents and making note of what facts still need checking. Within the [master] document, I have a column that tracks each fact to its checker's initials, whether an intern or producer. You refer back to that person if there are any questions later.\"\n\n\"Deposition videos taken under oath are powerful visual tools in investigative pieces. Even if you don't use them, transcripts of depositions and court testimony can be helpful in backing up facts.\"\n\nFact-checking is also a vital protection against liability and may ensure protection for the documentarian and her sources when the subject matter is sensitive. \"I did a film about minors facing abuse in the criminal justice system,\" Capps recalls. \"It can be difficult to have those conversations. But a lot of the stories that we told and the claims made were backed by legal documents and public records.\" Fact-checking can begin sometimes as early as the conception of the idea. \"Even before you begin your film or before an interview, it's useful to think about what documents or research will help to fact-check what the interviewees are saying or what the narration is going to be,\" says Capps.\n\n\"The fact-checker should be on board by the time the filmmaker is getting a cut together,\" advises Jones. \"I discuss the subject with the filmmaker, maybe watch a cut and set up a schedule of delivery dates. You don't want to be bugging the filmmaker every time you hit a road block on your facts or something needs to be changed. I get a feel for the script and its fairness and balance. Then I do what I call 'sweeps.' I underline all factual assertions made in the script, and begin to fact-check by accessing the source material. With Frontline, it's between one to three months [of fact-checking] before a piece locked. You are basically working backwards from when you want to be locked.\"\n\n--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nTechnical Advisor for Film: Fact-Check Brief | Gammal Tech (https://api.gammal.tech/doc/articles/technical-advisor-film-technology-fact-check.html)\nciteturn81search1 [wordlim: 200] Published: 2 weeks ago; Crawled: 2 weeks ago; # Technical Advisor for Film: A Scene Fact-Check Brief ... A fact-check is not a promise that every uncertainty disappears.\n## Run the final fact-check against the locked cut\n\nAt completion, watch and listen to the finished export with the claim ledger open. Check each item below:\n\n  * Every line of narration and every interview excerpt.\n  * Names, titles, pronouns, translations, dates, places, and quoted wording.\n  * Statistics, denominators, time periods, units, and the factual basis for causal language.\n  * Lower thirds, captions, maps, charts, labels, credits, and on-screen text.\n  * Photographs, archival clips, user-generated media, and the descriptions attached to them.\n  * Edits, music, sound bridges, reaction shots, and juxtaposition that may imply more than the words establish.\n  * Source notes and citations sufficient for another editor to locate the evidence.\n  * Permission, credit, and usage terms for third-party material.\n\nWhen the cut changes, repeat the affected checks. A new image can introduce a new claim; a shortened answer can remove a qualification; a revised lower third can turn an attributed statement into an apparently direct assertion.\n\nMaintain a corrections record after release. If an error is found, correct it promptly and clearly, with enough context for viewers to understand what changed. PBS standards call for prompt, clear corrections; the same discipline that documents the original check should document the correction.\n\n## A reusable decision sequence for every claim\n\n  1. Write the claim exactly as the audience encounters it.\n  2. Classify it. Is it personal experience, interpretation, hearsay, an external fact, an allegation, a causal explanation, or a visual assertion?\n  3. Identify the source’s position to know. Note whether the source observed the event, created the record, or repeats another account.\n  4. Test the source’s reach. What does it establish, and what does it not establish?\n  5. Seek appropriate corroboration. Use independent records, witnesses, experts, or original media, giving priority to consequential and disputed claims.\n  6. Search for contrary evidence and context. Check exceptions, missing time periods, later corrections, and competing explanations.\n  7. Choose the least misleading wording. Keep, qualify, attribute, add context, or remove.\n  8. Preserve the decision. Store the evidence, correspondence, timecode, and reason for the final treatment.\n  9. Recheck the locked edit. Confirm that picture, sound, graphics, and sequencing have not introduced a new implication.\n\nThis process makes the film more trustworthy without pretending that community history is simple. It gives viewers a clear account of what is documented, what is remembered, what is disputed, and what remains unknown.--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nWho Fact-Checks Documentaries? First determine whether the checks are answering the same proposition. A practical ledger combines FRONTLINE’s annotated-source requirement with the master-document approach described by IDA practitioners.\n\nField  | What to record\n--- | ---\nClaim text  | The exact sentence or a precise paraphrase; split compound sentences into separate claims.\nLocation  | Episode, script version, page, paragraph and final-audio timecode when available.\nSource and role  | Name, institutional or personal role, and whether the source is a witness, record custodian, expert or interested party.\nSource type  | Court filing, transcript, contemporaneous document, data, recording, direct observation, interview or secondary account.\nOriginal material  | Document title or recording identifier, page, timestamp, passage, file name and storage location.\nDate accessed  | When you obtained or reviewed the material; note revised versions.\nCorroboration  | Independent sources that support the same proposition and what each adds.\nContrary evidence  | Conflicting records, denials, alternative timelines or limitations you considered.\nStatus and caveat  | Unverified, supported, disputed, not established, or supported only as a person’s recollection; include wording restrictions.\nChecker and date  | The person who reviewed the claim and the date of the decision.\n\nDo not mark a claim “checked” merely because a link or article repeats it. The basics of good documentary research still apply: verify, compare, question, log, and check again.\n\nFor example, a study conducted by a pharmaceutical company on the effects of one of their drugs should be examined with much skepticism.\n\n## Be Aware Of Your Own Biases\n\nHave you already made up your mind about how your documentary is going end?\n\nHave you come up with pre-determined conclusions before investigating the facts?\n\nIf so, be aware of these biases and push yourself to seek alternative viewpoints.\n\nThe film (and your journey making it) will be much richer if you can approach the project with hyper curiosity and as an explorer on a mission of discovery.\n\n## Quick fact-checking checklist\n\nBefore you lock a claim into your treatment, script, voiceover, or final cut, ask:\n\n  * What exactly is the claim?\n  * What is the original source?\n  * Have I checked it against at least two more independent sources?\n  * Is this fact, opinion, allegation, or interpretation?\n  * Have I checked the quote, spelling, title, date, and context?\n  * If a visual is being used as evidence, have I verified the visual itself?\n  * If AI helped me find it, have I checked it outside the AI tool?\n\nAI can help you move faster and it can help you search more broadly and organise material more efficiently, but it cannot replace editorial judgment and fact-checking fundamentals.\n\nThe basics of good documentary research still apply: verify, compare, question, log, and check again.\n\nThat is how you protect your film, your subjects, and your credibility.\n\n## Resources:\n\nReuters Handbook of Journalism\n\nPBS Journalistic Guidelines\n\n'How Journalists Can Make AI Work For Them' article--------------------------------------------------------------------------------\nMapping the Documentary-Journalism Landscape — Method M Films (https://www.methodmfilms.com/map)\nciteturn81search11 [wordlim: 200] Crawled: today;
